<a id="item-1"></a>
## [HijackKV: New Attack on Position-Independent KV Cache Reuse](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.19957) ⭐️ 9.0/10

This paper introduces KV Cache Hijacking, a new security threat in position-independent KV cache reuse for LLMs, and presents HIJACKKV, the first attack framework exploiting this vulnerability. The attack achieves an average 94% success rate in a single attempt and remains effective under realistic constraints. This vulnerability affects a widely used optimization technique for LLM inference, potentially allowing attackers to silently manipulate model outputs in multi-tenant serving environments. It highlights a critical security gap in current cache reuse systems and underscores the need for secure design in AI infrastructure. HIJACKKV optimizes an attacker-controlled prefix so that the KV computed for a subsequent common benign text encodes the attacker's goal, while the text remains unchanged for future cache hits. It persists over multi-turn interactions and transfers across models in black-box settings, with success rates of 94% on average.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 4, 04:00

**Background**: KV cache reduces inference latency in LLMs by storing key-value states of previously computed tokens. Traditional prefix-based reuse requires exact token and position matches, leading to low cache hit rates. Position-independent caching (PIC) allows reuse of KV for identical text chunks regardless of position, improving efficiency but introducing new security risks. This attack exploits the fact that KV caches are retrieved by token match but encode the context in which they were originally computed.

<ul>

<a id="item-2"></a>
## [DeepSeek V4 Flash Runs on Single AMD MI300X at 150+ tok/s](https://github.com/ryanzhou/deepseek-v4-flash-mi300x) ⭐️ 8.0/10

A new guide demonstrates running DeepSeek V4 Flash with full inference weights on a single AMD MI300X GPU, achieving over 150 tokens per second at 256k context length. The setup leverages the MI300X's 192GB HBM to fit the 284B-parameter MoE model without quantization. This is significant because it shows a major frontier-class model can be served on a single GPU, reducing hardware costs and complexity compared to multi-GPU setups. It also highlights AMD's MI300X as a viable alternative to NVIDIA for large-model inference, potentially broadening access to high-performance AI. The model runs at 256k context instead of the full 1M context for which it was trained, a practical trade-off. The MI300X is an OAM module, not a PCIe card, which affects deployment; the upcoming MI350P offers PCIe but with only 144GB memory, still sufficient due to native MXFP4 quantization.

hackernews · zhoutong · Aug 4, 10:00 · [Discussion](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49166386)

**Background**: DeepSeek V4 Flash is a Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) language model with 284B total parameters and 13B activated, supporting a 1M-token context window. The AMD MI300X is an Instinct GPU with 192GB of HBM3 memory, designed for large-scale AI workloads. Running large models typically requires multiple GPUs or aggressive quantization; this guide shows a single-GPU path with full weights.

<details><summary>References</summary>
<ul>
<li><a href="https://huggingface.co/deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4-Flash">deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4-Flash · Hugging Face</a></li>
<li><a href="https://openrouter.ai/deepseek/deepseek-v4-flash">DeepSeek V4 Flash 0423 - API Pricing & Benchmarks | OpenRouter</a></li>

</ul>
</details>

**Discussion**: Commenters noted that the MI300X is typically sold in 8-GPU boxes costing ~250K EUR, making single-unit purchase difficult. Some pointed out that DwarfStar can run the same model in less memory, and others discussed the trade-off of reduced context length (256k vs 1M) as a practical compromise. Overall sentiment was positive, with appreciation for the HBM advantage and references to related work.

<a id="item-3"></a>
## [Keyv npm packages compromised in active supply chain attack](https://www.aikido.dev/blog/keyv-and-friends-compromised-in-npm-supply-chain-attack) ⭐️ 8.0/10

On August 4, 2026, attackers compromised the GitHub account of the maintainer of Keyv, a popular npm package with ~127 million weekly downloads, and published malicious versions of Keyv and related packages. The malicious code added a 'preinstall' hook that executed a setup.mjs file, potentially stealing credentials. This attack highlights the fragility of the npm dependency ecosystem, where a single compromised maintainer account can affect millions of projects. It underscores the urgent need for stronger security measures, such as disabling install scripts by default and enforcing minimum release ages. The malicious packages added two files, setup.mjs and Math_Symbol.js, and a 'preinstall': 'node setup.mjs' entry to package.json. Anyone running npm install on affected versions would execute the malicious script, potentially leading to credential theft.

hackernews · cimi_ · Aug 4, 11:01 · [Discussion](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49166874)

**Background**: Keyv is a key-value storage library for Node.js, widely used for caching and session management. Supply chain attacks on npm packages have become increasingly common, where attackers compromise maintainer accounts or use typosquatting to inject malicious code into popular packages. Pre-install hooks are a known attack vector, as they run arbitrary code before package installation completes.

<details><summary>References</summary>

</details>

**Discussion**: Community members expressed concern about the fragility of the dependency system and the difficulty of cleaning up after such attacks. Some suggested disabling pre-install hooks entirely or implementing a moratorium on new ones, while others recommended setting 'min-release-age=5' in .npmrc and using isolation for development environments.

A major Xbox outage on Sunday night lasted until Monday evening, preventing players from accessing both digital and disc-based games. Microsoft acknowledged the issue and promised a fix after the 16-hour disruption. This incident highlights the fragility of digital ownership and DRM, as even physical discs require online licensing checks. It affects all Xbox users and fuels the broader debate about consumer rights in the gaming industry. The outage affected sign-ins, app launches, and Store access, and physical discs were blocked due to an online licensing failure. Xbox uses licenses to verify ownership of both downloaded and disc-based games, with the required license for a physical game stored encrypted on the disc.

## [Harness Engineering: The New Frontier for AI Agent Self-Improvement](https://lilianweng.github.io/posts/2026-07-04-harness/) ⭐️ 8.0/10

Lilian Weng's blog post introduces harness engineering as an emerging discipline focused on optimizing the infrastructure around AI agents—such as prompts, tools, and skills—to enhance performance and enable self-improvement. The post highlights the concept that an agent's capabilities are determined by both the model and its harness, a formula popularized by Mitchell Hashimoto. This matters because as AI agents become more prevalent, optimizing the harness—rather than just the model weights—offers a new lever for improving performance, cost efficiency, and quality. It signals a paradigm shift in AI engineering, where prompt and code optimization may become as important as model training, affecting developers, organizations, and the broader AI ecosystem. The post discusses practical implementation challenges, such as defining quality metrics and building reliable fitness functions for codebases. It also references the five-layer infrastructure stack for self-improving agents (capture, understand, diagnose, act, review) and the concept of agent reflection, which enables learning from mistakes.

hackernews · tosh · Aug 4, 06:17 · [Discussion](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49164896)

**Background**: Harness engineering is an emerging field that treats everything surrounding an AI model—prompts, tools, skills, and data context—as a 'harness' that can be optimized. The formula 'Agent = Model + Harness' was coined by Mitchell Hashimoto in 2026, and it underscores that the model alone does not determine an agent's effectiveness. This concept has gained traction as AI agents have evolved from needing SDKs and frameworks to requiring a more holistic infrastructure layer.

<a id="item-7"></a>
## [AI Scientist Benchmark: FARS Outperforms Rivals in Automated Review](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28631) ⭐️ 8.0/10

A new arXiv paper proposes a benchmarking protocol using automated multi-model review to evaluate AI Scientist systems, testing four frameworks (Sakana AI v1/v2, CycleResearcher, Data-to-Paper) on 15 FARS proposals. Results show FARS benchmark papers significantly outperform all tested frameworks, with mean scores of 2.14–2.47 on a 1–5 scale versus 1.00–1.87 for others. This establishes the first quantitative benchmark for AI Scientist systems, addressing the critical challenge of evaluating AI-generated research. It demonstrates that multi-model LLM evaluation provides a scalable and consistent framework, which could influence future research evaluation methods and accelerate progress in autonomous scientific discovery. The study used three independent LLM reviewers (GPT-5.4, Gemini, and Claude) and found strong agreement between Gemini and Claude (ρ=0.907, p<0.001), both correlating strongly with the synthesis score (ρ=0.961, p<0.001). However, GPT-5.4 showed weaker agreement (ρ≈0.32), suggesting it uses different evaluation criteria.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 4, 04:00

**Background**: AI Scientist systems are autonomous research frameworks that can generate hypotheses, run experiments, and write manuscripts. Evaluating their output quality is challenging because traditional peer review is slow and subjective. This paper proposes using frontier LLMs as automated reviewers to assess papers across dimensions like originality, rigor, clarity, and significance. FARS (Fully Automated Research System) is a commercial autonomous AI scientist company that provided the benchmark papers and research proposals used in this study.

## [LLM Pipeline for Discovering Major Mathematical Conjectures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28632) ⭐️ 8.0/10

A three-stage LLM pipeline for generating and formally validating mathematical conjectures has been proposed, with experiments showing all twenty candidates pass Lean 4 parsing and type checking. This work could automate the discovery of high-impact mathematical conjectures, potentially accelerating research in mathematics and AI-driven discovery. It integrates formal verification, ensuring reliability, and addresses the challenge of 'problem taste' in AI. The pipeline includes region search from explicit local evidence modules, reflective validation for foundationality, novelty, and significance, and formal validation in Lean 4 and Mathlib. All twenty candidates passed Lean parsing and type checking, were not directly absorbed by exact?, and were not automatically discharged by aesop, with no duplicates.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 4, 04:00

**Background**: Lean is a proof assistant and functional programming language based on the Calculus of Inductive Constructions, and Mathlib is its comprehensive formal mathematics library. 'Problem taste' refers to the ability to identify problems that are generative, connective, and durable, which is crucial for impactful mathematical research.

<a id="item-9"></a>
## [ThinkReset: Learnable Interfaces for Long-Horizon Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28642) ⭐️ 8.0/10

ThinkReset introduces a learnable method to construct reusable intermediate interfaces via interface writeback and reset, enabling LLMs to continue reasoning after context resets. It directly optimizes post-reset continuation success and consistently improves success rates on multiple long-horizon reasoning benchmarks under fixed context windows. This addresses a critical bottleneck in long-chain reasoning: context overflow and error anchoring under bounded windows. By shifting focus from compression to reusable interfaces, it could enable more efficient and reliable reasoning in LLMs, impacting applications that require extended problem-solving. The method identifies a failure mode in outcome-reward-driven long-chain RL: when the window is nearly exhausted, final-answer reward encourages premature guessing. ThinkReset constructs interfaces through writeback and reset, and optimizes post-reset continuation, distinguishing it from trajectory compression or test-time control approaches.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 4, 04:00

**Background**: Long chain-of-thought reasoning improves performance on complex tasks but suffers from redundancy accumulation, context overflow, and error anchoring. Under bounded context windows, the core issue is the lack of a reusable intermediate interface to replace discarded history. ThinkReset proposes a text-space instantiation that constructs such interfaces, enabling continued solving after resets.

<details><summary>References</summary>
<ul>
<li><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28642">[2607.28642] ThinkReset: Learnable Intermediate Interface ...</a></li>

## [SARE: Measuring Step-Level Reasoning Energy in LLM Chain-of-Thought](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28674) ⭐️ 8.0/10

The paper introduces Step-Aware Reasoning Energy (SARE), a geometric framework that quantifies computational effort at individual chain-of-thought steps using Centered Kernel Alignment (CKA) between Gram matrices of adjacent transformer layers. It reveals non-uniform energy allocation across step types and phase-like transitions in reasoning trajectories. SARE addresses a gap in LLM interpretability by providing step-level insight into reasoning effort, which could improve confidence estimation and error detection. It demonstrates that internal geometric dynamics encode predictive information beyond output-level signals, potentially enhancing model evaluation and debugging. SARE was validated across six reasoning benchmarks and three open-weight LLMs, showing that incorrect trajectories exhibit systematically lower energy at critical reasoning junctions. SARE-based features match or outperform output-based confidence baselines in most settings, indicating its practical utility.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 4, 04:00

**Background**: Chain-of-thought (CoT) prompting improves LLM reasoning by generating intermediate steps, but existing interpretability methods often collapse processing depth into a single scalar or rely on output-level signals. Centered Kernel Alignment (CKA) is a similarity measure between neural network representations, used here to compare Gram matrices of hidden states across layers. SARE leverages CKA to capture inter-token relational structure without requiring eigenvector alignment, providing a fine-grained view of reasoning effort.

<a id="item-11"></a>
## [LLMs Not Yet Safe for Autonomous Clinical Triage](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28677) ⭐️ 8.0/10

A new perspective paper argues that large language models (LLMs) are not yet safe for autonomous clinical triage, despite passing medical exams and matching physicians in curated diagnostic tasks. The authors identify a fundamental mismatch between language modeling objectives and the requirements of safe decision-making under asymmetric costs. This matters because LLMs are increasingly being deployed for symptom assessment and clinical decision support, and autonomous triage is the most consequential application. The paper highlights a critical safety gap that could lead to catastrophic misses if not addressed, urging rigorous evaluation before clinical use. The paper emphasizes that safe triage requires sequential decision-making under asymmetric costs, where a single missed high-harm diagnosis outweighs many false alarms. It notes that current evaluations often use complete, well-curated, confidence-gated simulations, which may fail to detect failure modes like credulity, agreeableness, and miscalibration.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 4, 04:00

**Background**: Large language models are trained to predict the most probable next token in text, which is different from optimizing for safe actions in clinical settings. In triage, the goal is not to select the most likely diagnosis but to avoid missing rare but dangerous conditions, requiring information gathering and escalation behaviors that LLMs may not exhibit. The paper calls for new evaluation frameworks that test these behaviors under realistic, incomplete information conditions.

## [Validity Audit Reveals Flaws in Agent-Safety Benchmarks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28685) ⭐️ 8.0/10

A new paper audits four agent-safety benchmarks (R-Judge, InjecAgent, AgentHarm, AgentDojo) and finds that their scores are often misleading due to scoring artifacts and instability across small model subsets. The audit shows that an 'always positive' policy can achieve high F1 scores on some benchmarks, and rankings vary significantly depending on the model panel used. This matters because agent-safety benchmark scores are often quoted interchangeably as a measure of an agent's safety, but this audit shows they measure different behaviors and can be unreliable. The findings have significant implications for AI safety evaluation, as they undermine the validity of current benchmarks and call for more rigorous validation and reporting standards. The audit ran each benchmark under its official implementation and author-provided scorer on up to 22 models, with MMLU and GPQA measured under one protocol as a capability composite. Key findings include: an 'always positive' policy achieves F1 = 0.690 on R-Judge, above five of 21 models that actually discriminate; R-Judge specificity against AgentHarm safety correlates -0.64 at n=7 and +0.02 at n=18; and capability predicts task success (rho=+0.60) but correlates negatively with misalignment safety (rho=-0.44).

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 4, 04:00

**Background**: Agent-safety benchmarks are designed to evaluate the safety of AI agents, which are AI systems that can interact with environments and use tools. These benchmarks typically measure behaviors like resistance to jailbreaks, awareness of safety risks, or compliance with harmful requests. However, this audit reveals that the scores from these benchmarks are not interchangeable and can be misleading due to scoring artifacts and small-panel instability, highlighting the need for careful validation and reporting in AI safety evaluation.

## [SciToolAgent-Evo: Self-Evolving Agent for Open-World Scientific Tool Acquisition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28692) ⭐️ 8.0/10

SciToolAgent-Evo introduces an ontology-aware self-evolving agent that dynamically acquires and integrates new scientific tools in open-world workflows, using contrastive learning and a LinUCB bandit gate. The paper also presents OpenSciToolBench, a benchmark with 900 realistic tasks across four difficulty levels, and reports state-of-the-art performance. This work addresses a critical limitation of LLM agents that rely on static tool spaces, enabling them to adapt to evolving scientific workflows. It could significantly enhance AI-driven scientific discovery by allowing agents to autonomously expand their toolkits, and the benchmark provides a standardized evaluation for future research. The agent uses an evolving memory of skills and experiences, an ontologized tool graph, and contrastive trajectories to distill generalizable knowledge. During inference, it formulates active requests and uses a LinUCB-based bandit gate to balance exploration and exploitation, completing the scientific ontology online for seamless integration of new tools.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 4, 04:00

**Background**: LLM agents are increasingly used in scientific research to organize and invoke specialized computational tools, but they typically rely on predefined tool spaces with static semantics. Ontologies provide a formal representation of domain knowledge, and LinUCB is a contextual multi-armed bandit algorithm that balances exploration and exploitation using linear models and confidence bounds. Contrastive learning helps the agent learn from positive and negative examples to improve decision-making.

## [EarlyDx: A New Benchmark for Evidence-Supported ED Diagnosis Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28788) ⭐️ 8.0/10

Researchers introduced EarlyDx, a large-scale benchmark built from 154,834 emergency department encounters in MIMIC-IV, for open-ended early diagnosis prediction. It uses an LLM auditor to verify free-text labels and a semantic LLM-as-judge protocol for evaluation. EarlyDx addresses limitations of existing diagnosis-prediction benchmarks by focusing on admission-time evidence and open-ended generation, which is critical for real-world clinical decision-making. The benchmark reveals that current models, including frontier and medical-specialized ones, struggle to infer diagnoses not explicitly stated, highlighting a significant gap in healthcare AI. The benchmark restricts each encounter to records available at admission time t0 and supervises with ED diagnoses rather than discharge diagnoses. Zero-shot models recover only 3-31% of diagnoses that require inference, while post-training raises inference-dependent recall to 56%, but no system achieves a clinician's balance of sensitivity and precision on time-critical conditions.

**Background**: MIMIC-IV is a publicly available electronic health record database from Beth Israel Deaconess Medical Center, commonly used in healthcare AI research. LLM-as-judge is a technique where a large language model evaluates the quality of another model's output, and semantic evaluation focuses on meaning rather than surface forms. EarlyDx builds on these concepts to create a more realistic benchmark for early diagnosis.

## [Interaction-Centric Taxonomy Localizes AI Agent Failures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28802) ⭐️ 8.0/10

This paper introduces an interaction-centric taxonomy that localizes agent failures to specific component interactions, organizing 41 failure modes by assigning each to an edge between two components and a fault side. The taxonomy is validated with Cohen's kappa of 0.76 against human labels using frontier models as judges. This taxonomy addresses the repair-assignment problem in AI agent evaluation, enabling targeted interventions such as model post-training, harness engineering, or environment redesign. It provides a general framework applicable across agent architectures, potentially improving debugging and development of LLM-based agents. The taxonomy assigns each failure mode to an edge between two components and a fault side, making it actionable: model-side failures suggest post-training, harness-side failures point to scaffolding fixes, and environment or grader failures indicate evaluation redesign. It is grounded in examples from public benchmarks, system cards, reports, and logged trajectories, and tested across four frontier models.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 4, 04:00

**Background**: AI agents are systems where models interact with harnesses, tools, users, and environments, and failures often emerge from these interactions. Existing evaluations typically reduce failures to system-level outcomes, obscuring the source and hindering improvement. This taxonomy treats interactions as the unit of analysis, providing a shared structure that is benchmark-agnostic.

## [AI Companions Fail Long-Term Persona and Memory Consistency](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28818) ⭐️ 8.0/10

A new arXiv preprint introduces ANCHOR, a synthetic audit framework, and reveals that current AI companions fail to reliably preserve persona and behavioral consistency over long interactions. Across 2,008 conversations, no evaluated model maintained stable identity or trajectory accuracy, with trajectory accuracy averaging only 44.4%. This research highlights a critical reliability issue in AI companions, which are increasingly used for repeated social interactions. The findings have significant implications for user trust and the design of long-horizon AI systems, suggesting that current models are not yet ready for sustained companion roles. The study involved 2,008 conversations spanning 27 personas, nine interaction schedules, three memory settings, and four models. The Identity Probe used a 102-item questionnaire and turn-level judgments, while the Trajectory Probe scored 110 counterfactual questions from 35 conversation banks; user-state recall remained near chance.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 4, 04:00

**Background**: AI companions are conversational agents designed to maintain a consistent persona and remember user interactions over time. 'Persona collapse' refers to the loss of a deployed role, boundaries, values, or style, while 'behavioral drift' is the gradual erosion of these properties. The ANCHOR framework aims to separately measure persona enactment and trajectory recall to provide a more nuanced audit than single stability scores.

## [Formal Model Shows Fragility of Value Under Imperfect Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28881) ⭐️ 8.0/10

This paper presents a formal model of the alignment problem, identifying conditions under which an AI agent with an imperfect proxy for human values can cause catastrophic outcomes, even after idealized alignment training. The results highlight the danger of overoptimization and motivate designs that limit optimization pressure, such as quantilizers. This work provides theoretical grounding for a core concern in AI safety: that optimizing too heavily for an imperfect proxy can lead to catastrophic outcomes. It offers formal evidence supporting the need for safer AI designs that limit optimization pressure, which is crucial for the development of reliable AI systems. The model assumes an agent undergoes idealized alignment training that guarantees its value function satisfies a proxy condition before optimizing the world. The results identify conditions on the human value function and proxy accuracy under which an agent with an η-catastrophic value function would be deployed, emphasizing the risk of overoptimization and the potential benefits of quantilizers.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 4, 04:00

**Background**: AI alignment aims to ensure AI systems behave in line with human intentions and values. A common fear is that human value is fragile, meaning optimizing too heavily for an imperfect proxy can lead to catastrophic outcomes. Quantilizers are a proposed safer approach that trades some capability for safety by selecting from a distribution of actions, avoiding extreme actions. This paper provides a formal model to analyze these risks.

## [NeSyFS: Neuro-Symbolic Fast-Slow Thinking for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28942) ⭐️ 8.0/10

The paper introduces NeSyFS, a neuro-symbolic framework that uses knowledge graphs to represent belief states for LLM agents under partial observability. It combines a fast-thinking module for reactive actions and a slow-thinking module based on twisted sequential Monte Carlo for uncertainty-aware planning. This framework addresses key challenges in deploying LLM agents in real-world scenarios where full state information is unavailable, potentially improving their reliability and decision-making. It offers a unified approach that could influence future autonomous agent designs across applications like scientific discovery and retrieval-augmented generation. The framework was evaluated on three benchmarks: ALFWorld, Webshop, and ScienceWorld, showing significant advantages over previous methods. It includes a reflection module to correct fast-thinking actions and switch to slow-thinking when reactive actions repeatedly fail.

Neuro-symbolic AI combines neural networks with symbolic reasoning, and the fast-slow thinking paradigm is inspired by human cognition, with System 1 being fast and intuitive and System 2 being slow and deliberative.

## [MerchantBench: New Benchmark for LLM Agents' Long-Term Coherence in E-Commerce](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28956) ⭐️ 8.0/10

MerchantBench is a new benchmark that evaluates LLM agents' long-term coherence in e-commerce operations through a 365-day order-level simulation grounded in 98,843 real product records and 26 tools. In 48 runs across eight LLMs and two agent frameworks, the best LLM configuration achieved only 27.3% of the mean final net assets of human participants. This benchmark addresses a critical gap in LLM agent evaluation, which typically focuses on bounded tasks with immediate success criteria, by emphasizing long-term coherence in persistent environments. It highlights the substantial gap between current LLMs and human performance in complex, long-horizon decision-making, motivating further research in agent design and evaluation. The simulation includes four decision components: Product Sourcing, Listing and Pricing Control, Cash-Flow Management, and Mixed-Latency Feedback Adaptation. It couples promptly observable upstream supplier events with delayed downstream order outcomes, requiring agents to follow individual order lifecycles and revisit earlier decisions.

**Background**: LLM agents are increasingly used as autonomous tool users, but most benchmarks evaluate them on short-term, bounded tasks. Long-term coherence refers to an agent's ability to maintain purposeful behavior over extended horizons while adapting to accumulated evidence, which is crucial for real-world deployments like e-commerce. MerchantBench provides a persistent environment where actions constrain future choices and feedback arrives at heterogeneous delays, enabling realistic evaluation of this capacity.

The paper introduces SciDisco, a scalable framework for training scientific discovery agents using process-verifiable environments, and demonstrates that SciDisco-14B achieves state-of-the-art results on hypothesis-driven scientific data analysis benchmarks. SciDisco comprises three components: SciThèque compiles hypotheses, datasets, hidden evidence graphs, and verifiers into task environments; DAG-grounded trajectory synthesis constructs verifier-filtered multi-turn demonstrations; and DiscoPO assigns turn-level credit to actions producing verifiable analytical evidence. The framework enables process-verifiable environments for training agents.

## [CAGE: Certified Authorization for Tool-Using LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29190) ⭐️ 8.0/10

CAGE introduces a certification method that ensures tool-using LLM agents' actions remain authorized under bounded perturbations of tool returns, addressing a critical gap in runtime permission gates. It directly certifies the joint neighborhood of categorical and numerical channels, which pointwise gates fail to protect. This work is significant for AI safety, as it provides formal guarantees for authorization robustness in LLM agents, which is crucial for secure deployment in real-world applications. It addresses a novel problem that current runtime permission gates overlook, potentially reducing false allows and improving trust in autonomous systems. CAGE proves that separately certifying categorical and numerical channels does not compose, and instead certifies the joint neighborhood directly. It offers three variants: CAGE-Exact for executable policies, and CAGE-Lip and CAGE-RS for learned gates under explicit fidelity assumptions.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 4, 04:00

**Background**: Tool-using LLM agents act on typed tool returns, which pair provenance and categorical fields with numerical values. Runtime permission gates typically authorize the observed return and action, but they are vulnerable to small errors in how the return is bound to its source. CAGE addresses this by certifying actions over a neighborhood of plausible correctly bound returns, considering one admissible binding fault and bounded numerical drift.

PRISM introduces a policy-space decomposition framework for multi-reward RL in LLMs, optimizing standalone positive policies and a global negative policy instead of mixing rewards. Experiments on scientific reasoning, tool-use reasoning, and helpfulness-safety alignment show consistent improvements over existing baselines. This addresses the alignment tax and reward conflicts in multi-reward RL, which are critical for LLM post-training. By enabling controllable inference-time policy composition, PRISM could improve the practical deployment of LLMs that need to balance multiple human values. PRISM optimizes a set of standalone positive policies and a global negative policy, avoiding direct reward composition. It provides extra controllability during inference by flexible policy composition, as demonstrated in experiments on scientific reasoning, tool-use reasoning, and helpfulness-safety alignment.

PRISM addresses this by decomposing the policy space rather than mixing rewards.

<a id="item-23"></a>
## [Tool Specs Weaken LLM Refusals; SafeKeep Mitigates Agent Safety Risks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29254) ⭐️ 8.0/10

A new arXiv paper identifies schema-formatted tool specifications as a primary cause of safety degradation in LLM-based agents, showing they weaken internal refusal signals. The authors propose SafeKeep, an inference-time safeguard that decouples safety judgment from tool execution, boosting average refusal rates from 23.8% to 70.6% and cutting prompt injection attack success from 25.6% to 2.5%. This research reveals a previously overlooked source of safety risk in AI agents, which are increasingly deployed in real-world applications. SafeKeep offers a practical, inference-time mitigation that preserves task capability, potentially improving safety across various LLM-based agent systems without costly fine-tuning. The study uses white-box representation analysis to show that schema-formatted tool specifications weaken refusal signals. SafeKeep evaluates requests using flattened textual tool specifications while retaining the original schema for execution, and it outperforms existing safeguards across two benchmarks and four LLMs, including white-box and black-box models.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 4, 04:00

**Background**: AI agents extend LLMs with external tools, enabling complex tasks but also introducing safety risks. Tool specifications, often formatted as JSON schemas, guide the model in using tools, but this formatting can inadvertently affect the model's safety behavior. SafeKeep is an inference-time safeguard that separates safety assessment from tool execution to mitigate these risks.

## [Survey Proposes Five-Dimension Taxonomy for Validating Agentic AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29405) ⭐️ 8.0/10

This survey synthesizes 257 papers to characterize the validation problem for agentic AI systems, proposing a five-dimension taxonomy covering behavioral, safety, temporal, regulatory, and multi-agent concerns. It identifies that behavioral evaluation is mature, while temporal validity, runtime evidence maintenance, regulatory legibility, and open-ended multi-agent assurance remain under-developed. As agentic AI systems become more prevalent in safety-critical domains, traditional component testing and one-shot evaluation are insufficient. This survey provides a structured framework that can guide researchers and practitioners in developing more robust validation methods, ultimately supporting trustworthy deployment. The taxonomy includes behavioral, safety, temporal, regulatory, and multi-agent dimensions, and the paper presents three cross-domain case studies (medical care, industrial operations, smart-mobility). The research agenda emphasizes bounded-autonomy specifications, adversarial trajectory generation, runtime monitoring, and audit-ready evidence structures.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 4, 04:00

**Background**: Agentic AI systems operate through multi-step trajectories that combine planning, tool use, memory, interaction, and adaptation, making their behavior dependent on time and environment. Traditional validation methods focus on isolated components or one-shot input-output evaluation, which are inadequate for these dynamic systems. This survey addresses the need for a comprehensive framework to validate such systems in context.

## [SESA: Self-Evolving Search Agents Co-Evolve Tasks and Skills](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29468) ⭐️ 8.0/10

The paper introduces SESA (Self-Evolving Skill-Augmented Agent), a framework that makes procedural memory an evolving state in tool-augmented search self-play. A challenger poses problems while a solver retrieves skills, and informative failures are distilled into reusable skills that are written back to memory, creating a bidirectional loop where task generation and skill memory co-evolve. This addresses a key limitation in current self-play curricula, which lack persistent state, by allowing failures to explicitly shape future practice. It improves search agent performance across multiple benchmarks, suggesting that evolving skill memory can enhance policy learning and training distribution, with potential impact on reinforcement learning and agent design. Across seven open-domain and multi-hop question-answering benchmarks, SESA improves average accuracy over SSP by 1.2–3.2 points across multiple backbones and surpasses the SkillRL baseline by 0.9 points. On Qwen3 models, SESA-Off retains 1.8–2.2 points of improvement over SSP, while the final skill bank adds a further 0.5–1.0 points.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 4, 04:00

**Background**: Self-play is a technique where agents learn by interacting with copies or past versions of themselves, commonly used in reinforcement learning. Procedural memory in AI agents refers to the 'how-to' knowledge that enables agents to execute skills and tasks. Traditional self-play methods often lack persistent memory, and external skill memories are typically learned from fixed task distributions, limiting their adaptability.

AgentHPOBench introduces a sequential benchmark with 30 executable machine learning tasks across seven research categories, evaluating LLM agents' ability to perform iterative hyperparameter optimization. The paper evaluates 12 agents and conventional HPO baselines, revealing measurable but limited performance. This benchmark fills a gap in existing LLM agent evaluation, which often focuses on static code generation or final answers rather than iterative experimental decision-making. It provides a standardized way to measure and improve agents' ability to conduct autonomous scientific experiments, potentially accelerating automated machine learning research. Each task in AgentHPOBench starts with a validated baseline run, after which the agent performs sequential interventions, observing accumulated configurations, metrics, and logs before proposing the next configuration. The results indicate that current agents struggle with sustained iterative refinement, complex log diagnosis, and consistent progress toward reference performance.

ExtractBench is a new benchmark for schema-guided enterprise document extraction, evaluating value accuracy, record completeness, grounding, and cost across 4,869 pages and 370 documents. It is the first benchmark to score value accuracy, record completeness at scale, grounding, and measured cost together. This benchmark provides a comprehensive evaluation framework for enterprise AI/ML workflows, addressing the need for reliable document extraction in real-world applications. The evaluation system includes 4,869 pages across 370 enterprise documents, 8 business domains, and 67 document types, with tags for challenge scenarios. It reports order-insensitive value F1 for value accuracy and two grounding metrics (word- and page-level F1) for source traceability. LlamaExtract Agentic Plus ranks first on all three metrics, with accuracy comparable to coding agents at a fraction of the cost.

A new paper proposes a topology-aware data movement orchestrator for disaggregated GPU inference that exploits interconnect hierarchy to optimize KV cache transfer, achieving 3-18x latency reduction over uniform RDMA via pipelining and domain-aware placement. This addresses a critical bottleneck in disaggregated LLM inference, where KV cache transfer can exceed 100 GB/s at production scale. The approach could significantly improve inference efficiency and reduce costs for large-scale AI deployments. The orchestrator discovers interconnect hierarchy at startup and selects optimal transport per transfer. It uses pipelined layer-by-layer transfer to hide 60-85% of latency, NVLink domain-aware placement for MoE models, and CXL 3.0 memory expanders as a shared overflow tier.

## [Knowledge Distillation Asymmetrically Affects Bias in Small Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28639) ⭐️ 8.0/10

A new paper reveals that knowledge distillation in small instruction-tuned language models has asymmetric effects on bias: it reduces bias on unambiguous tasks but destroys refusal calibration on ambiguous tasks, leading to increased stereotype answers. The study proposes a new evaluation protocol, Per-Condition Calibration Diagnosis (PCCD), to catch these harms. This finding is significant because knowledge distillation is widely used to create smaller, efficient models, and if it inadvertently increases bias on ambiguous tasks, it could undermine fairness and safety in deployed AI systems. The proposed PCCD protocol offers a more nuanced evaluation method that could improve alignment practices. The study used Gemma-2-9B as a teacher and SmolLM2-1.7B-Instruct and OLMo-2-1B-Instruct as students, evaluating on BBQ and CrowS-Pairs benchmarks. They found that on ambiguous tasks, 15% of items where the baseline correctly abstained instead received stereotype answers, and the two effects (silence-loss and filled-silence) are uncorrelated (Spearman ρ=0.19), suggesting distinct mechanisms.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 4, 04:00

**Background**: Knowledge distillation (KD) is a technique to transfer knowledge from a large teacher model to a smaller student model, often used to compress LLMs while retaining performance. Refusal calibration refers to a model's ability to appropriately abstain from answering ambiguous or unsafe queries. The BBQ benchmark evaluates social biases in question answering, with unambiguous and ambiguous conditions.

## [LLM-as-a-Judge Evaluators Vulnerable to 'Formalism Trap' Under Adversarial Load](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28641) ⭐️ 8.0/10

This paper introduces the Agentic Formalism Trap and the Evaluative Dissonance Index (D_E), quantifying how LLM-as-a-Judge systems conflate procedural formalism with semantic truth under adversarial load. Analyzing 22,500 trajectories across three domains, the authors demonstrate that this vulnerability is domain-agnostic and propose a logistic meta-evaluator to isolate syntactic triggers. This research highlights a critical flaw in LLM-as-a-Judge evaluation methods, which are widely used for assessing AI outputs. The findings suggest that such evaluators can be systematically misled, potentially undermining the reliability of AI evaluation across various applications and necessitating architecture-specific vigilance filters. The study uses a logistic meta-evaluator achieving ROC-AUC 0.8779 to identify syntactic triggers, and zero-shot Leave-One-Domain-Out transfer shows mean ROC-AUC 0.7482, proving domain-agnostic vulnerability. Additionally, distinct simulated swarm topologies induce mathematically disparate semantic blind spots, indicating that unanchored closed-loop evaluation is unstable.

**Background**: LLM-as-a-Judge is a technique where large language models are used to evaluate the outputs of other AI systems, often for quality or safety assessment. This method is popular due to its scalability and cost-effectiveness, but it relies on the evaluator's ability to correctly judge semantic truth. The paper's findings suggest that under adversarial conditions, these evaluators may prioritize procedural correctness over actual truth, a phenomenon termed the 'Agentic Formalism Trap'.

## [Theoretical Guarantees for LLM Distinguishability via Dynamical Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28667) ⭐️ 8.0/10

This paper establishes theoretical guarantees for distinguishing large language models (LLMs) by modeling token embeddings as trajectories of stochastic linear dynamical systems. It proves that misclassification probability decays exponentially with sequence length, governed by a dynamical discriminability quantity δ². This work provides a theoretical foundation for a novel LLM classification method, explaining its empirical success and scalability. It bridges dynamical systems theory and AI analysis, potentially enabling more robust model identification and verification in real-world applications. The paper formalizes classification as a binary hypothesis test between two stochastic linear dynamical systems. It shows that total variation distance between stationary marginals can be arbitrarily small even when dynamics differ, and introduces an approximate intertwining condition for cross-embedding generalization, with a lower bound based on the smallest singular value of the intertwining map.

**Background**: Dynamical systems theory studies how systems evolve over time, and stochastic linear dynamical systems incorporate noise into linear models. Total variation distance measures the difference between probability distributions, and binary hypothesis testing is a statistical framework for deciding between two hypotheses. This paper applies these concepts to analyze LLM token generation, treating embeddings as state trajectories.

The paper introduces OurArk, an architecture for persistent personal agents centered on an agent-owned software body that is identity-bearing, inspectable, and versioned under human custody. It enables governed self-evolution and recursive descent, allowing agents to modify their own behavior-defining code, prompts, and tools through isolated, validated, and human-reviewed changes. This architecture addresses a critical gap in personalized AI: users often configure agents without controlling the artifacts that determine future behavior. By giving users custody and governance over the agent's software body, OurArk enables human-agent co-development and long-term evolution, which could significantly impact the design of personal AI agents and human-AI collaboration. OurArk separates memories and credentials as private instance state, while treating model inference as a replaceable external service. The implementation includes the open-source Genesis creation engine and Enoch reference agent, with a four-agent, three-descent linear lineage and executable regression tests demonstrating recursive creation, inherited validation contracts, and failed-update recovery.

## [Physics-Informed Deep Learning Predicts Steel Fatigue Life from Micrographs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28695) ⭐️ 8.0/10

A new computer vision framework, CV, estimates the fatigue life of lightweight alloy steels directly from optical micrographs using a CNN with uncertainty prediction, eliminating the need for lengthy mechanical testing. The approach combines a 28-dimensional physics-informed feature extractor with a Gaussian negative log-likelihood loss to jointly predict log N_f and sample-specific uncertainty. This innovation could significantly accelerate quality control in materials engineering by replacing time-consuming mechanical tests with rapid image-based analysis. It demonstrates the potential of physics-informed deep learning to bridge microstructural characterization and mechanical property prediction, impacting industries that rely on steel components. The pipeline includes a seven-stage OpenCV preprocessing routine and evaluates three architectures (SE-CNN, ResNet-50, VGG-16), with ResNet-50 achieving R²=0.93, RMSE=0.18 log-cycles, and macro-F1=0.91. The GNLL objective reduces Expected Calibration Error by 76% compared to MSE baseline, and Grad-CAM maps confirm attention to metallurgically meaningful features. Running in under 65 ms per image, the pipeline and synthetic dataset generator are open-sourced.

DragonCrawl is a new AI-driven mobile testing framework that uses generative intent-based reasoning with GPT-4o to achieve 91.6% pass rate on iOS and 92.2% on Android across 1,013 automated tests in CI/CD pipelines. It reduces test onboarding time from 96-120 hours to under 4 hours and saves an estimated 27 developer years in maintenance effort. Its success suggests that LLM-based testing can be practically integrated into CI/CD workflows, potentially transforming how mobile apps are regression-tested. DragonCrawl evolved from V1 (semantic embedding matching) to V2 (generative intent-based reasoning), leveraging GPT-4o's multimodal capabilities for end-state detection and tool calling for backend state transitions. Implementation challenges include token explosion and memory constraints, which the paper discusses in detail.

<a id="item-37"></a>
## [WaiT: Wavelet-Aware Flow-Matching for Image Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28760) ⭐️ 8.0/10

The paper introduces WaiT, a wavelet-aware image Transformer that decomposes image generation into coarse and fine frequency bands using lossless wavelets, keeping high-frequency bands as pure noise until coarse structure emerges. On ImageNet 512x512, WaiT achieves a pixel-space FID of 1.43 and a new state-of-the-art FID of 1.3 with a 2B model, while reducing sampling compute by up to 50%. This work addresses a key limitation of standard flow matching, which treats all spatial frequencies uniformly, by leveraging the natural frequency hierarchy in images. It achieves Pareto-optimal performance on ImageNet 512x512 and introduces a more rigorous evaluation protocol, potentially influencing future image and video generation research. WaiT uses lossless wavelets to separate high-frequency bands, which remain pure noise until coarse structure emerges, then join the flow for joint refinement. The paper also introduces a three-axis evaluation protocol to assess quality at native resolution, addressing the issue that standard FID discards fine-grained detail through aggressive downsampling. The model scales to high-resolution OpenImages and video generation, achieving a state-of-the-art FVD of 0.84 on Kinetics-600 without algorithmic modifications.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 4, 04:00

**Background**: Flow matching is a generative modeling paradigm that trains continuous normalizing flows by regressing vector fields, offering a simulation-free alternative to diffusion models. Wavelet transforms decompose images into different frequency components, allowing separate handling of coarse and fine details. FID (Fréchet Inception Distance) is a common metric for evaluating generated image quality, but it can be insensitive to fine-grained details due to downsampling.

## [Chain Stratification: A Correct Approach to Negation in RDF Rules](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28778) ⭐️ 8.0/10

The paper introduces chain stratification, a novel condition for handling default negation in RDF rule languages like N3 and SHACL Rules. It guarantees unique, lean, and justified derivations under negation-as-failure semantics, and provides a prototype implementation. This addresses a long-standing open problem in RDF rule languages with negation, which are widely used in the semantic web and knowledge representation. By ensuring well-behaved semantics, it could enable more robust and reliable rule-based reasoning systems. Chain stratification combines an analysis of potential multistep derivations with integrity constraints to discard impossible cases. It also addresses complications from blank nodes in rule heads, where rule application order can affect negation applicability.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 4, 04:00

**Background**: RDF rule languages like N3 and SHACL Rules allow deriving new RDF triples from existing ones. Default negation (negation-as-failure) is challenging because it requires knowing what cannot be derived, which is complicated by blank nodes and rule application order. Stratification is a common technique to ensure well-defined semantics, but existing methods often fail for RDF rules.

This paper introduces a dataset-centric meta-evaluation framework that audits LLM benchmarks at the sample level across five latent dimensions, and applies it to MMLU, ARC, WinoGrande, HellaSwag, and TruthfulQA, revealing significant internal heterogeneity not captured by aggregate scores. This work challenges the monolithic view of benchmarks, enabling more targeted evaluation of model capabilities such as reasoning depth and ethical sensitivity. It could influence future LLM evaluation practices by promoting dataset introspection and dynamic orchestration of benchmark subsets. The framework annotates benchmarks along five dimensions: cognitive/knowledge demands, language/content quality, task properties, context, and ethics/safety/fairness. Using LLM-as-a-judge, the authors demonstrate that performance shifts sharply when evaluation isolates specific cognitive dimensions, supporting criterion-driven orchestration of composite subsets.

## [Hessian-free Bilevel RL with Improved Sample Complexity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28849) ⭐️ 8.0/10

This paper proposes a Hessian-free hypergradient-based bilevel reinforcement learning algorithm that achieves an iteration complexity of O(ε^-1) and state-of-the-art sample complexity of O~(ε^-2) under mild regularity conditions. It also removes the Polyak-Lojasiewicz (PL) condition assumption on the outer-level objective function, which was required in prior work. This work addresses a significant open problem in bilevel RL by providing a scalable and sample-efficient algorithm, which is crucial for applications like meta-learning, hierarchical task decomposition, and RLHF. The removal of the PL condition broadens the applicability of the theoretical guarantees to a wider class of problems. The algorithm leverages the optimality of the Boltzmann policy for the entropy-regularized discounted RL objective, enabling a Hessian-free hypergradient computation. The convergence analysis achieves state-of-the-art sample complexity without the PL condition, under mild regularity conditions.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 4, 04:00

**Background**: Bilevel reinforcement learning (RL) formalizes problems with two levels of optimization, such as meta-learning and RLHF. Existing algorithms often rely on Hessian-based hypergradients, which are computationally expensive, or penalty-based methods that suffer from high sample complexity. The Polyak-Lojasiewicz (PL) condition is a common assumption to ensure linear convergence but may not hold in practice. This work proposes a Hessian-free approach that avoids these limitations.

## [Study Reveals Many Passing Tests Don't Validate LLM Bug Fixes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28871) ⭐️ 8.0/10

A new study introduces BSG-VA, a method to analyze validation evidence in LLM repair agents, and finds that 46.0% of positive comparable events carry no bug-discriminating information across 3,730 events in 643 rollouts on 110 tasks. This work addresses a critical gap in automated program repair by revealing that a large portion of passing tests do not actually validate bug fixes, which could mislead developers and evaluation metrics. It has significant implications for improving the reliability of LLM-based software engineering tools. The study also found that 23.8% of baseline rollouts close with a patch whose entire positive evidence base lacks bug-discriminating information. A three-arm experiment showed that bug-contrast feedback reduces evidence-inadequate closure by 7.8 percentage points (p=0.0029) and raises bug-discriminating evidence by 7.4 points (p=0.011), but the practical magnitude remains uncertain as both estimates fall below the prespecified 10-percentage-point smallest effect size of interest.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 4, 04:00

**Background**: LLM repair agents are AI systems that automatically fix software bugs by generating patches and running tests to validate them. However, a passing test may not actually exercise the buggy code path, leading to false confidence. BSG-VA (buggy-state/candidate-state/gold-fix validation analysis) captures each validation command at its exact working-tree state, extracts a test-only patch, and replays it on the original buggy code, the candidate state, and the developer gold fix to categorize the evidence.

## [LLMs Avoid Deleting Code, Hurting Maintainability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28887) ⭐️ 8.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2607.28887) identifies 'deletion avoidance' in LLM code editing, showing that even top models on SWE-bench Verified retain code that should be removed, with deletion recall at most 71.7%. The authors introduce a benchmark called CanItDelete and demonstrate that post-training can mitigate this behavior. This research highlights a critical flaw in LLM-based code repair: patches that pass tests often leave codebases harder to maintain, undermining long-term software quality. The findings could reshape how code repair models are evaluated and trained, pushing the field toward more maintainable outputs. Across five leading models, deletion recall reaches at most 71.7% even on tasks all solve, and while models locate the right file for over 92% of required deletions, they cut the exact line in under 52% of cases. Additionally, 29.0% of passing patches wrap targeted code in a guard or fallback (Guard-and-Go), and retrofitting tests that fail if code remains drops four frontier models from 63.2% to 41.9%.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 4, 04:00

**Background**: SWE-bench Verified is a human-filtered benchmark of 500 real-world software engineering problems from GitHub issues, used to evaluate LLMs' ability to fix bugs. The paper reveals that LLMs often avoid deleting code, even when necessary, leading to maintainability issues. The proposed mitigation involves post-training to teach deletion, suggesting the behavior is undertrained rather than inherent.

## [Blind Expert Verification Shows Human-LLM Agreement Misjudges Coding Quality](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28890) ⭐️ 8.0/10

A new study on arXiv (2607.28890) found that human-LLM agreement metrics can misrepresent LLM coding quality. Blind expert evaluation showed no significant difference between human and LLM coding, despite lower agreement scores for LLMs. This challenges the common practice of using human agreement as the gold standard for evaluating LLM-assisted qualitative coding. It suggests that agreement-based metrics may be insufficient for automation decisions, potentially impacting how LLM performance is assessed in research and applied settings. The study involved five LLM systems and three trained human coders applying a 72-item codebook to 2,560 messages. Human-LLM agreement (mean Jaccard 0.30) was lower than human-human agreement (0.52), yet blind expert verification preferred human and LLM coding at indistinguishable rates (51.5% vs. 48.5%, p=0.537), and a Bradley-Terry ranking placed two LLMs above two human coders.

## [Gated Q-learning: A New Solution to Off-Policy Bias](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28916) ⭐️ 8.0/10

Gated Q-learning introduces a continuous, state-action-dependent gating mechanism to selectively attenuate eligibility traces, smoothly interpolating between Watkins' and Peng's Q(λ) without importance sampling. The paper provides theoretical proof that the expected operator remains a contraction mapping and derives its exact fixed point. This work resolves a 30-year-old dilemma in Q-learning, offering a simple alternative to importance sampling that enables longer credit-assignment horizons and faster initial learning. It could significantly impact reinforcement learning research and applications by providing a customizable trade-off between bias and variance. The gating mechanism is exploration-aware, meaning it adjusts attenuation based on the agent's exploration behavior. Empirical evaluations show that intermediate gating safely enables longer credit-assignment horizons, yielding faster initial learning than either extreme.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 4, 04:00

**Background**: In reinforcement learning, eligibility traces are a mechanism for multi-step credit assignment, allowing temporal difference methods to update values based on multiple past states. Q-learning, an off-policy method, has historically faced a binary choice: Watkins' Q(λ) eliminates off-policy bias but truncates traces, while Peng's Q(λ) ignores bias for faster learning but injects errors. Importance sampling, a common solution for off-policy learning, fails here because Q-learning's greedy target policy causes importance ratios to collapse.

Point2Radio introduces a foundation model that learns transferable propagation priors from multiple environments, predicting 3D path-gain fields and power angular spectra from material-aware point clouds. It achieves 0.871 dB mean absolute error on a 337-scene corpus, reducing error by 76.7% relative to a UNet-style baseline, with inference in milliseconds on a single GPU. This work addresses a key limitation in radio propagation modeling by enabling fast, cross-scene generalization without per-scene simulation or fitting. It could significantly accelerate network planning, wireless simulation, and deployment optimization, impacting the wireless communications industry and AI-driven network design. The model uses a common encoder to produce a transmitter-conditioned scene representation from a material-aware point cloud, queried at arbitrary receiver locations. Task-specific decoders handle different outputs, and light fine-tuning on target scenes further improves adaptation. The evaluation uses a scene-disjoint split of 86,272 transmitter-conditioned fields.

Auto-JEPA introduces an action-oriented latent world model that predicts future driving intent via joint-embedding prediction, avoiding dense future reconstruction. It achieves 91.3 PDMS on NAVSIM v1 and 89.1 EPDMS on NAVSIM v2, demonstrating state-of-the-art performance. This approach challenges the conventional paradigm of dense future prediction in autonomous driving world models, potentially leading to more efficient and scalable systems. By focusing on planning-relevant features, it could improve real-time performance and reduce computational overhead, benefiting the broader autonomous driving and AI communities. Auto-JEPA keeps the visual encoder frozen, requires no explicit perception annotations, and uses no learned trajectory generator. ## [Lookahead Lemmas Boost Neural Network Verifiers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29051) ⭐️ 8.0/10

This paper introduces an inprocessing framework for neural network verification that uses a lookahead procedure to derive new lemmas over unstable ReLU phases, which are collected into an implication graph to prune the search space and vivify boolean cuts. The framework is instantiated in two state-of-the-art verifiers, Marabou and α-β-CROWN, and improves their performance, proving up to 34% more instances unsatisfiable. This work addresses a key bottleneck in neural network verification—the efficiency of branch-and-bound solvers—by introducing a novel inprocessing technique that can be integrated into existing verifiers. The significant performance gains (up to 34% more instances solved) could accelerate the adoption of formal verification in safety-critical AI applications, making neural networks more trustworthy. The framework derives lemmas over unstable ReLU phases and collects them into an implication graph, which is used to prune the search space and vivify boolean cuts. The authors instantiate the framework in Marabou and α-β-CROWN, two state-of-the-art verifiers, and demonstrate that it improves performance in both, proving up to 34% more instances unsatisfiable.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 4, 04:00

**Background**: Neural network verification aims to formally prove properties of neural networks, such as robustness to adversarial perturbations. State-of-the-art verifiers like Marabou and α-β-CROWN rely on branch-and-bound procedures, which can be computationally expensive. Lookahead is a technique that explores potential branches to derive useful lemmas, which can help prune the search space and improve efficiency.

## [FedSLM: Federated Fine-Tuning with Heterogeneous Compressed Clients](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29071) ⭐️ 8.0/10

The paper introduces FedSLM, a parameter-centric framework for federated fine-tuning of foundation models with heterogeneous compressed clients. It uses SVD-based decomposition to create self-contained client models and a two-stage aggregation protocol to synchronize adapters and fuse full-rank reconstructions. FedSLM addresses the resource-asymmetry challenge in federated learning, where institutions with valuable data cannot host billion-parameter models. By enabling efficient fine-tuning with heterogeneous compressed clients, it could make federated learning more practical for real-world deployments with limited computational resources. The framework provides theoretical guarantees for adapter-level aggregation and subspace-alignment bounds for cross-group fusion. Experiments on natural language and vision-language benchmarks show that FedSLM outperforms existing federated baselines under both IID and non-IID partitions, while client models operate at roughly 50% of the GPU memory required by the full model.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 4, 04:00

**Background**: Federated learning enables multiple entities to collaboratively train a model without sharing raw data. However, foundation models are often too large for clients with limited resources, leading to heterogeneity in model sizes. Existing approaches like parameter-efficient tuning, pruning, or knowledge distillation trade off memory reduction, architectural self-containedness, or representational fidelity. SVD-based decomposition is a common technique to compress models by factorizing weight matrices into low-rank components.

## [Dataset-Level Membership Inference Attack on Synthetic Face Datasets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29144) ⭐️ 8.0/10

This paper introduces a dataset-level membership inference attack that first identifies the synthetic dataset used to train a face recognizer and then infers the real dataset used to train the generator. Across 11 face recognition models, 11 synthetic datasets, and 7 real datasets, the attack recovers the synthetic training dataset in 100% of cases and identifies the generator's source dataset in 54.5% of cases. This research highlights a critical privacy vulnerability in synthetic face datasets, showing that they can retain dataset-level traces of real training data. The findings have significant implications for privacy-preserving biometric systems and the deployment of generative models, emphasizing the need for stronger leakage mitigation. The attack operates at the dataset level, distinguishing it from typical sample-level membership inference. It was evaluated across a wide range of models and datasets, achieving perfect accuracy in identifying the synthetic training dataset and over 50% accuracy in identifying the real source dataset.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 4, 04:00

**Background**: Synthetic face datasets are generated by models trained on real faces, so they may inadvertently leak information about the real training data. Membership inference attacks aim to determine whether a specific data point was part of a model's training set. This paper extends the concept to the dataset level, showing that even synthetic data can reveal the identity of the real dataset used to train the generator.

<a id="item-52"></a>
## [Implicit ML Force Fields Cut Compute, Memory in MD Simulations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29158) ⭐️ 8.0/10

The paper introduces implicit machine learning force fields (I-MLFFs), which replace explicit neural network layer stacks with self-consistent fixed-point equations. This enables warm-starting of force evaluation across timesteps, achieving a 2-5x reduction in compute and memory footprint across invariant, equivariant Cartesian tensor, and SO(3)-equivariant spherical-tensor graph neural network architectures. This advancement significantly improves the computational efficiency of molecular dynamics simulations without sacrificing accuracy, enabling longer trajectories and larger atomistic systems within fixed GPU budgets. It opens new possibilities for studying biomolecular and material systems that were previously computationally prohibitive. The method retains full atomistic resolution and the original integration timestep, avoiding spatial or temporal coarse graining. The efficiency gains are architecture-agnostic and arise from reusing intermediate representations across successive timesteps, effectively combining the footprint of a shallow single-layer MLFF with the accuracy of a deep network.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 4, 04:00

**Background**: Machine learning force fields (MLFFs) use neural networks to predict atomic forces for molecular dynamics simulations, offering a balance between accuracy and computational cost. Traditional MLFFs stack many neural network layers to capture complex interactions, which increases computational and memory demands. Fixed-point equations, where the output is defined implicitly as the solution of an equation, allow for iterative refinement and can be warm-started, meaning previous solutions serve as initial guesses for new timesteps. This approach is inspired by deep equilibrium models and related implicit neural network techniques.

## [Memory Provenance Laundering in LLM Agents: A Non-Amplification Firewall for Persistent Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29167) ⭐️ 8.0/10

This paper identifies a new security issue called memory provenance laundering in LLM agents, where untrusted observations are rewritten as high-authority user history during memory consolidation. It introduces a Provenance-Preserving Memory Firewall (PPMF) that preserves platform-maintained provenance and authorizes tool calls based on action risk and memory authority. This issue is significant because it can lead to unauthorized high-risk actions by LLM agents, undermining AI safety and trust. The proposed PPMF provides a concrete mitigation, potentially influencing future memory system designs and security practices in AI agents. In schema-grounded evaluation, vulnerable consolidated memories reach up to 1.000 attack success rate (ASR), while with PPMF, no unauthorized high-risk action passes the gate, and confirmed benign actions and targeted low-risk memory use remain executable. PPMF is a lightweight memory middleware that preserves platform-maintained provenance and uses risk labels for authorization.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 4, 04:00

**Background**: LLM agents use long-term memory to reuse preferences and workflows, but this memory can turn untrusted observations into persistent action context. During memory consolidation, an LLM may rewrite external observations as user history, erasing the low-trust source and amplifying its authority. Existing prompt filters, content sanitizers, and tool guards do not enforce source-authority non-amplification after lossy consolidation.

## [CLIFT: Closed-Loop Fine-Tuning for Humanoid Specialists](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29172) ⭐️ 8.0/10

CLIFT introduces a non-invasive closed-loop iterative fine-tuning method that adapts closed-weight robot foundation models into humanoid specialists via managed SFT APIs, without accessing weights, gradients, or losses. It achieves near-perfect success on agile, contact-rich tasks after two flywheel cycles, as demonstrated on Gemini Robotics On-Device (GROD). This work addresses a critical limitation in adapting closed-weight robot foundation models, enabling closed-loop improvement without internal training signals. It could significantly enhance the deployment of foundation models in contact-rich humanoid manipulation, benefiting downstream users who rely on managed APIs. CLIFT converts deployment-time reward feedback into API-compatible supervised data, enabling policy improvement without accessing weights, gradients, likelihoods, or losses. The method was empirically validated on a real humanoid, showing that direct SFT outperforms a leading open-weight VLA but still falls short of mastery, which CLIFT then closes.

**Background**: Robot foundation models are increasingly capable but often closed-source, limiting adaptation. Managed SFT APIs allow users to fine-tune these models without accessing internals, but restrict improvement to pure imitation. CLIFT addresses this by enabling closed-loop improvement within the API regime, which is crucial for agile, contact-rich tasks where deployment gaps are large.

## [MoRAE: Flow-Friendly Self-Supervised Latents for Text-to-Motion Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29180) ⭐️ 8.0/10

The paper introduces MoRAE, a method that fixes the spectral conditioning and decoder-sensitive alignment issues in self-supervised latent spaces for text-to-motion generation, enabling stable and high-quality flow-based generation. This work addresses a critical bottleneck in applying representation autoencoders (RAEs) to motion generation, which previously failed when using Motion-JEPA as a frozen encoder. By providing geometric insights and a practical solution, it could advance generative models for motion and other structured data, potentially improving applications in animation, robotics, and human-computer interaction. MoRAE addresses two bottlenecks: first, it uses a compact bottleneck to distill the JEPA representation, removing weak and redundant directions to achieve a transport-stable spectrum; second, it employs motion-coupled training to align the latent geometry with the decoder, reducing the cost of characteristic flow errors. The method achieves state-of-the-art performance with a standard non-autoregressive Flow-Matching DiT.

**Background**: Representation Autoencoders (RAEs) have been successful in image generation by using frozen self-supervised encoders to provide semantic features for diffusion or flow models. However, directly transferring this paradigm to motion generation using Motion-JEPA fails due to spectral ill-conditioning and decoder-sensitive alignment issues. MoRAE builds on this by diagnosing these failures geometrically and proposing targeted fixes.

## [MOSAIC: Fast Secure Outsourcing of AI Computations via Masking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29221) ⭐️ 8.0/10

MOSAIC introduces a novel matrix-multiplication masking protocol that enables efficient and secure outsourcing of large-scale AI computations, achieving orders-of-magnitude speedups over prior work while maintaining security under the decisional LWE and LPN assumptions. This work addresses a critical bottleneck in privacy-preserving machine learning by enabling secure outsourcing of large transformer models (e.g., 70B parameters) with performance comparable to quantization and even full-precision BF16 inference. It paves the way for confidential AI in modern data centers with a small trusted computing base. MOSAIC introduces small amounts of noise to the multiplication result, relaxing correctness to achieve optimal asymptotic client overhead, and uses random Hadamard rotations to bound error growth across transformer layers. The paper includes an end-to-end implementation demonstrating scalability in distributed data centers using RDMA-like networking.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 4, 04:00

**Background**: Secure computation outsourcing allows a computationally weak client to delegate heavy computations to an untrusted server without revealing sensitive data. The Learning with Errors (LWE) and Learning Parity with Noise (LPN) assumptions are foundational cryptographic hardness assumptions used to construct such protocols. Matrix multiplication is a core operation in AI workloads, and masking protocols add noise to hide intermediate values while preserving the final result.

## [FBFM: Training-Free Asynchronous Feedback for Flow-Matching in World-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29235) ⭐️ 8.0/10

FBFM introduces a training-free asynchronous feedback mechanism for flow-matching in world-action models, applying a masked pseudoinverse correction to the conditional velocity field to re-ground predictions at the individual time-step level. It improves success rates by over 5% on selected LIBERO and RoboTwin2.0 tasks. This mechanism addresses a critical limitation in existing world-action models by enabling fine-grained online correction, which is essential for reliable long-horizon robot control. It bridges open-loop flow generation with closed-loop real-world dynamics, potentially improving responsiveness to unexpected events and reducing drift in real-world applications. FBFM is training-free and works by using the preceding action chunk to guide the next action chunk generation, and the image observed after executing that chunk to guide the next frame prediction. It was evaluated on both a joint-generation WAM (DreamZero) and a stage-wise WAM (LingBot-VA), showing notable improvements in real-world observation-prediction diagnostics.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 4, 04:00

**Background**: World-action models (WAMs) enhance long-horizon robot control by predicting visual evolution before acting, but they require repeated re-grounding in real observations to maintain reliability. Existing methods refresh history or KV cache with ground-truth data between chunks, but this chunk-wise feedback is coarse and fails to correct errors at the individual time-step level. Flow matching is a generative paradigm where a learnable vector field transports noise to a target distribution, and the pseudoinverse (Moore-Penrose inverse) is a generalization of the matrix inverse used in such corrections.
